Spinor Representations for Fields with any Spin: Lorentz Tensor Basis for Operators and Covariant Multipole Decomposition
Wim Cosyn, Frank Vera

Abstract
This paper discusses a framework to parametrize and decompose operator matrix elements for particles with higher spin ( j > 1 / 2 ) (j>1/2) using chiral representations of the Lorentz group, i.e. the ( j , 0 ) (j,0) and ( 0 , j ) (0,j) representations and their parity-invariant direct sum. Unlike traditional approaches that require imposing constraints to eliminate spurious degrees of freedom, these chiral representations contain exactly the 2 ​ j + 1 2j+1 components needed to describe a spin- j j particle. The central objects in the construction are the t t -tensors, which are generalizations of the Pauli four-vector σ μ \sigma^{\mu} for higher spin. For the generalized spinors of these representations, we demonstrate how the algebra of the t t -tensors allows to formulate a generalization of the Dirac matrix basis for any spin. For on-shell bilinears, we show that a set consisting exclusively of covariant multipoles of order 0 ≤ m ≤ 2 ​ j 0\leq m\leq 2j forms a complete basis. We provide explicit expressions for all bilinears of the generalized Dirac matrix basis, which are valid for any spin value. As a byproduct of our derivations we present an efficient algorithm to compute the t t -tensor matrix elements. The formalism presented here paves the way to use a more unified approach to analyze the non-perturbative QCD structure of hadrons and nuclei across different spin values, with clear physical interpretation of the resulting distributions as covariant multipoles.



Preprint:
JLAB-THY-25-4259

I
Introduction

While the Standard Model only contains particles with spin 0,1/2 and 1, hadronic and nuclear bound states exhibit a much richer spin content, with spins larger than 1. To quote two examples with large spin values, the most abundant isotope of Mendelevium has spin 8
[
1
]
and the
\Delta(2950)
is a spin-15/2 resonance
[
2
]
. A field-theoretical treatment of these higher-spin objects requires covariant fields, wave functions and wave equations with spin
>
1/2.

The relativistic treatment of higher-spin particles has a long history
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
. The traditional method to deal with spin
j>1/2
one-particle states uses covariant objects which contain more than
2j+1
degrees of freedom, i.e. a four-vector
\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda)
for spin 1, a vector-spinor Rarita-Schwinger fermion for spin 3/2
[
5
]
, etc. Spurious degrees of freedom are eliminated by imposing constraints on the wave functions in the form of “equations of motion”, see e.g. Ref.
[
6
]
for a review of the spin-3/2 case. General constructions for the wave functions are based on tensor products of four-vectors, belonging to Lorentz group representations of the form
(1/2,1/2)
, with Dirac bispinors, belonging to Lorentz group representations of the form
(1/2,0)\oplus(0,1/2)
. As spin values increase, more constraints must be imposed, such as:

1.

\left(p\hskip-5.0pt/-m\right)\psi(p,\lambda)=0
, for the case of fermion fields with spin 1/2 and above.

2.

\ p_{\mu}\epsilon^{\mu}(p,\lambda)=0
, for the case of fields with spin-1 and above.

3.

\ \gamma^{\mu}\psi_{\mu}(p,\lambda)=0
, for the case of fields with spin-3/2 and above.

4.

etc…

Maintaining such constraints consistently can be challenging in both analytic and numerical calculations (see e.g. Refs.
[
7
,
8
]
), and often leads to spurious singularities in amplitudes when using conventional constrained fields. A Lagrangian formulation for these Rarita-Schwinger fields was constructed by Singh and Hagen using auxiliary fields
[
9
,
10
]
.

Alternative approaches exist for treating higher-spin particles. A
(j_{1},j_{2})
-representation of the Lorentz group contains spins
|j_{1}-j_{2}|,\cdots,j_{1}+j_{2}
. Consequently, any
(j_{1},j_{2})
-representation for which
|j_{1}-j_{2}|\leq j\leq j_{1}+j_{2}
can be used to represent spin-
j
particles. The simplest of these are the so-called maximally chiral representations
(j,0)
and
(0,j)
, which contain the minimal
2j+1
components to describe a spin-
j
particle and all spin degrees of freedom reside in a single chiral sector.
Therefore, these representations do not require additional constraints to eliminate spurious components. These chiral fields (and their parity-invariant direct sum
(j,0)\oplus(0,j)
) were developed and studied a long time ago
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, but building consistent Lagrangians or field equations with them proved difficult
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
.
We note, however, that the interest in maximally chiral representations has persisted. Spinor-helicity methods based on the spin-1/2 chiral spinors have flourished in perturbative QCD and modern on-shell amplitude theory, and have been extended to
massive
higher-spin states
[
18
]
. They have been successfully applied in the context of the Standard Model and beyond Standard Model
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
, in electroweak processes
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
, as well as more recent work on massive higher-spin EFTs
[
25
,
26
]
. By contrast, in the context of non-perturbative applications in hadronic and nuclear physics their use
has remained comparatively rare.

We want to stress that our interest here is not the construction of a(n effective) field theory based on the chiral representations, but their use in matrix elements of QCD operators for (massive) hadrons and nuclei. These matrix elements are used to parametrize the non-perturbative partonic (spin) structure of QCD bound states. Based on Lorentz covariance, they can be decomposed and parametrized with independent wave function bilinears, each multiplied by Lorentz invariant functions. The invariant functions contain information about the non-perturbative structure of these strongly interacting bound systems (hadrons, nuclei). A classic example would be the parametrization of the hadron current in elastic electron-nucleon scattering:

\displaystyle\sum_{f}e_{f}\langle\,p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\,|\,\bar{q}_{f}(0)\gamma^{\mu}q_{f}(0)\,|\,p,\lambda\,\rangle=\;F_{1}(Q^{2})\,\bar{u}(p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\gamma^{\mu}u(p,\lambda)+F_{2}(Q^{2})\bar{u}(p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\frac{\text{i}\sigma^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}}{2m}u(p,\lambda)\,,

(1)

where
e_{f}
is the fractional charge of quark flavor
f
,
m
the hadron mass,
Q^{2}=-(p^{\prime}-p)^{2}=-q^{2}
.
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are the invariant Dirac and Pauli nucleon electromagnetic form factors and
\sigma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{2}[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]
. A more contemporary pinnacle of this approach would be the off-forward spin-1/2 matrix element of a bilocal light-ray operator that parametrizes quark and gluon generalized transverse momentum distributions (GTMDs)
[
27
]
.

For hadrons of higher spin, similar efforts have focused on the spin 1 and spin 3/2 cases, given their phenomenological interest (vector mesons, deuteron nucleus, baryon decuplet). The main approach to parametrize these matrix elements has been to parametrize the bilinears using the four-vectors for spin 1 and Rarita-Schwinger vector-spinor for spin 3/2. The decomposition is essentialy carried out again for each spin case and operators separately, see Refs.
[
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
]
for some examples. For these higher spin cases, the physical interpretation of the different Lorentz invariant distributions becomes less clear. For instance, the multipole structure of different distributions is not immediately evident and requires additional case-by-case analysis.

We show here that the use of the chiral representations can pave the way to a more unified approach. For our purposes, the hadron states in these matrix elements can be considered as asymptotic states and the question of a consistent Lagrangian for the chiral fields is not relevant at this point. As we show in this article, which lays out the foundations of the framework, one obtains essentially a generalization of the well-known Dirac algebra for the spin-1/2 case. While this has been presented in the past
[
14
,
33
]
, our presentation here is a more intuitive and practical one which includes explicit expression for the spin-
j
bilinears. Moreover, we show that a natural basis for the on-shell spin-
j
bilinears is built from two separate towers (even/odd) of covariant
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
multipoles, this builds on work explored by Cotogno et al. in Ref.
[
34
]
.

The generalized Dirac algebra structure has the advantage that one can use the accrued intuition from the spin-1/2 case and apply it to any higher spin. With the breakdown in covariant multipoles, it becomes immediately clear which structures appear in identical form in the lower spin cases, and which structures are new due to the increased spin degrees of freedom. As such, a decomposition with a natural physical interpretation for the distributions or form factors can be obtained in a straightforward manner. Additionally, the use of chiral spinors that do not require any additional constraints can improve the analyticity properties of these matrix elements, important for numerical implementations. All this combined makes for an attractive, systematic and unified procedure to decompose the matrix elements for hadrons and nuclei with higher spin.

This article provides all the details about the basic construction of the framework culminating in the expressions for all gamma matrix basis bilinears. Subsequent work will apply this formalism to specific operators.
As the chiral spinors are central to our framework but not commonly used, we start with a review of the chiral fields formalism in Section
II
. After a brief overview of the irreducible representations of the Lorentz group, we focus in the left and right chiral representations, denoted as
(j,0)
and
(0,j)
, and the corresponding causal fields. The
t
-tensors are discussed, which are the generalization of Pauli four-vector
\sigma^{\mu}
to any spin. These tensors are central to the framework and can be used to build the spinors and propagators for massive particles of any spin
[
13
]
.
We discuss the
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
parameters that can be used to generate the standard helicity and light-front spinors; these parameters and their use in this context have, to our knowledge, not been presented before. Here, the
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
are a 4-tuple that package the effect of the standard boost, and contracting
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
with the
t
-tensors result in the chiral spinors, see Eqs. (
47a
) and further.

Background on the helicity and light-front spinors is included in Appendix
B
. We illustrate in Fig.
1
how the
t
-tensors relate to all the different objects used in the formalism and the corresponding sections of the paper. We hope that this helps clarify the overall structure and interconnections in this article. Section
II
concludes by introducing the generalized Dirac gamma matrices which will enter as part of the independent bilinears later.

Figure 1:
Flowchart illustrating the structure of the paper and formalism. Equations are meant to be read as schematic here. Equation numbers link to the full expressions. Arrows labeled with
\mathsf{P}
stand for parity-invariant extensions that combine a left- and right-chiral representation. From the way the oval blob connects to many other parts of the flowchart, one can appreciate the central role the
t
-tensors play in the construction.

We then move on to present the new results developed in this work:

1.

In Sec.
III
, we show that the rank-
2j

t
-tensors contain a basis for Hermitian matrices of order
2j+1
(the
\mathfrak{u}(2j+1)
algebra), which can be used to parametrize any Hermitian operator matrix element for spin-
j
particles, including all the QCD operators of interest. We include in this section an efficient algorithm to construct the
t
-tensors for any spin, based on the fact that in a lightcone-spherical basis all the matrices contained within the
t
-tensors have exactly one non-zero matrix element. A second algorithm to construct them is included in Appendix
C
, which is based on the anticommutators (Jordan algebra) of the rotation matrices. This algorithm was one we arrived at in the early stages of this work and is less efficient than the one presented in Sec.
III
. We choose to include it however, because it helps elucidate some aspects of the
t
-tensors, which can be hard to grasp at first.

2.

The massive spinor-helicity (MHS) formalism of Ref.
[
18
]
also uses objects that reside in the maximally chiral representations and is widely used in high-energy physics. Consequently there should be a map between MSH and the chiral spinors considered here. This is detailed in Section
IV
, in addition to illustrating that all the different types of chiral spinors used here also obey massive spinor-helicity-like equations.

3.

Section
V
contains the most important results in this work.
Products of
t
-tensors naturally appear in calculations with chiral (bi)spinors and the generalized gamma matrices.
Consequently their algebra is of interest. In Sec.
V.1
, we focus first on the cubic product of
t
-tensors which is shown to be irreducible and can be written as an invariant linear combination of
t
-tensor elements for any spin:

\displaystyle t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\bar{t}^{\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{2j}}t^{\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{2j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[(2j)!]^{2}}\underset{\left\{(\rho),(\sigma)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\sigma_{l}\alpha_{l}}\right)t_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2j}}\,,

(2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu\rho\sigma\alpha}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\rho}g^{\sigma\alpha}-g^{\mu\sigma}g^{\rho\alpha}+g^{\mu\alpha}g^{\rho\sigma}+{\rm i}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma\alpha}\,.

(3)

The proof of this result is presented in Appendix
D
. Next, in Sec.
V.2
, we focus on the quadratic products of
t
-tensors, which are shown to be reducible. The quadratic products contain a series of bi-indexed tensors (having symmetrized pairs of two antisymmetric indices) that transform covariantly within themselves and turn out to be in one-to-one correspondence to
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
multipoles, see Sec.
V.3
. These results for the
t
-tensor algebra are then used in Sec.
V.4
to identify a basis for the generalized Gamma matrices in a manner that exhibits this multipole structure. Useful expressions and relations for the coefficients that appear in the algebra of the
t
-tensors are collected in Appendices
E
and
F
.

4.

As a practical application of the derived results, we present expressions for all bilinears using the gamma matrix basis in Sec.
VI
. The expressions are valid for the different types of spinors (canonical, helicity, light-front) and can be used in further studies of QCD operator matrix elements for particles of any spin. Additionally, we discuss on-shell identities in Sec.
VII
. These allow us to further reduce the number of independent bilinears that needs to be used in the decomposition of matrix elements to only those corresponding to the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
multipoles.

We wrap up the paper in Sec.
VIII
with a discussion of our results and outlook for future extensions and applications. As the work presented here is quite technical and the details of the results heavily depend on the conventions in notation and definitions, we summarize all our choices in the dedicated Appendix
A
. We encourage readers to consult it before delving into the detailed derivations.

II
Review of the construction for general spinors

The goal of this section is to provide a self-contained and pedagogical review and summary of Weinberg’s construction for spinor representations of any spin for massive particles
[
13
]
.
Parts of this construction are anticipated in the work of Joos
[
11
]
(generalized Dirac equation) and Barut-Muzinich-Williams
[
12
]
(intertwining maps between spinorial and Lorentz tensor representations for any spin). This section serves as a reminder of the properties of the Lorentz group, its chiral and bispinor representations, causal fields and propagators. For spin 0 and spin 1/2, the construction corresponds to the well-known formalism associated with Klein-Gordon, respectively Weyl and Dirac fields, but for spin
j>1/2
the use of these chiral and bispinor representations is not common.
If the reader is familiar with the chiral spinor construction, they can skip ahead to the next section, although Sec.
II.3
does include some results for the values of parameters
\tilde{p}
for helicity and light-front spinors which to our knowledge have not appeared elsewhere.

II.1
Irreducible representations of the Lorentz group

The starting point to discuss the finite-dimensional irreducible representations of the Lorentz group are the well known commutation relations for the
\mathfrak{so}(3,1)=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
algebra of the homogeneous Lorentz group:

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{J}_{l},\mathbb{J}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm i}\epsilon_{lmn}\mathbb{J}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{J}_{l},\mathbb{K}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm i}\epsilon_{lmn}\mathbb{K}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{K}_{l},\mathbb{K}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=-{\rm i}\epsilon_{lmn}\mathbb{J}_{n}\,.

(4)

Here

\mathbb{J}_{i}
are the generators of rotations and
\mathbb{K}_{j}
are the generators of pure (rotationless) boosts. The Lorentz algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
can be complexified as
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{C}
by introducing

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{m}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{J}_{m}+{\rm i}\mathbb{K}_{m})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{m}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{J}_{m}-{\rm i}\mathbb{K}_{m})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{m}=\mathbb{A}_{m}+\mathbb{B}_{m}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{m}=-{\rm i}(\mathbb{A}_{m}-\mathbb{B}_{m})\,.

(5)

Using the commutation relations of Eq. (
4
) leads to the following commutation relations for the generators
\mathbb{A}_{m},\mathbb{B}_{n}
:

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{A}_{l},\mathbb{A}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm i}\epsilon_{lmn}\mathbb{A}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{B}_{l},\mathbb{B}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm i}\epsilon_{lmn}\mathbb{B}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{A}_{l},\mathbb{B}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6)

This shows that
\mathbb{A}_{m}
and
\mathbb{B}_{m}
form two commuting subalgebras. Each subalgebra obeys the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
commutation relations. The Lie algebra direct sum decomposition
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{so}(3,1)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\mathbb{C}}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\mathbb{C}}
is thus obtained. The irreducible representations (irreps) of SL(
2,\mathbb{C})
, the double cover of the Lorentz group, are labeled by a pair of heighest weights
(j_{A},j_{B})
that identify the corresponding
\mathfrak{su}(2)
irreps of the two subalgebras. While the finite-dimensional representations of the
\{\mathbb{A}_{m},\mathbb{B}_{m}\}
algebras are unitary, the factor i in the equation for
\mathbb{K}_{m}
in Eq. (
5
) shows that finite dimensional representations of the boosts will not be unitary. However, the rotations remain unitary. This is also reflected in the non-compact nature of the homogeneous Lorentz group (boost parameters are not bound, while rotation parameters are).

Of particular focus in Weinberg’s construction and the work presented here are the chiral representations of the Lorentz group. These chiral representations correspond to the irreps labeled as
(j,0)
, denoted
left-handed
, and
(0,j)
, denoted
right-handed
. This choice of handedness follows the standard convention. They are particular as they transform as singlets under one of the two
\mathfrak{su}(2)
subalgebras, which leads to the following relation between the chiral
representations

[\mathbb{J}_{m}],[\mathbb{K}_{m}]
of the generators of pure boosts and rotations:

left-handed
(j,0)
:

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt[\mathbb{K}_{m}]\equiv-{\rm i}[\mathbb{J}_{m}]\,,

(7a)

right-handed
(0,j)
:

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt[\mathbb{K}_{m}]\equiv+{\rm i}[\mathbb{J}_{m}]\,.

(7b)

In Table
1
, we give an overview of the objects that correspond to the representations with the smallest
(j_{A},j_{B})
values. Although most of these objects are commonly used in covariant calculations, the spin-1 chiral spinors corresponding to
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
are not commonly used. Objects like these will be the focus of this work.

(j_{A},j_{B})

Representation

(0,0)

Scalar

(\frac{1}{2},0)

Left-handed Weyl spinor, defining SL(
2,\mathbb{C}
) representation

(0,\frac{1}{2})

Right-handed Weyl spinor

(\frac{1}{2},0)\oplus(0,\frac{1}{2})

Dirac spin-1/2 bispinor

(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})

Four-vector, defining SO(3,1) representation

(1,0)

Anti-self-dual 2-form

Left-handed spin-1 spinor

(0,1)

Self-dual 2-form

Right-handed spin-1 spinor

(1,0)\oplus(0,1)

Parity-conserving 2-form (e.g.
F_{\mu\nu}
)

Spin-1 bispinor

Table 1:
Summary of the lowest dimensional representations of the Lorentz group.

II.2
Chiral representations

In Ref.
[
13
]
, Weinberg used the chiral irreducible representations to construct causal fields for massive particles.
These fields are unitary and infinite-dimensional representations of the Poincaré group. He derived their Feynman rules and provided expressions for their propagators and spinors. As the chiral spinors are objects that contain the minimal number of degrees of freedom (
2j+1
for a spin-
j
particle), these fields do not obey any free equation of motion besides the Klein-Gordon equation, which is satisfied for each field component. This fixes the on-mass shell condition
p^{2}=m^{2}
for the massive particle.
Feynman rules and interacting Lagrangians for maximally chiral fields have been constructed in other contexts as well
[
24
,
25
]
, though these are not our focus here.
In what follows, we summarize the most important results that Weinberg derived in Ref.
[
13
]
, specific details can be found in that reference.
1
1

1

It is worth emphasizing that the construction for massless particles and fields proceeds differently because of their different little group
[
35
]
. For theories with long-range interactions, Weinberg in Ref.
[
36
]
recovers gauge invariance and the Maxwell (massless spin-1) or Einstein (massless spin-2) equations. For a recent chiral formulation that includes massive and massless particles see Ref.
[
18
]
.

For the chiral representations of Eq. (
7
), rotations
R
take the form of the well-known Wigner
D
-matrices

D^{(j)}[R]=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[R]=e^{-{\rm i}\bm{\theta}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\,,

(8)

where in general
D^{(j)}{[\Lambda]}
(
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{[\Lambda]}
) represent the left-handed (right-handed) irreducible matrix representation in dimension
2j+1
of Lorentz transformation
\Lambda
,
e.g.
, rotations in the case above.
The
{J}^{(j)}_{i}
are the
(2j+1)
-dimensional representation of the generators of rotation in the basis of states with spin projections
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}

\left({J}^{(j)}_{i}\right)_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}=\langle j,\lambda^{\prime}|\mathbb{J}_{i}|j,\lambda\rangle\,.

(9)

If spin is quantized along the
z
-axis, these correspond to the familiar Pauli matrices for spin 1/2, the generators of rotations in the spherical basis for spin 1, etc.

Applying Eq. (
238
) to the left- and right-handed chiral representations of pure boost transformations, we obtain using Eqs. (
7
)

\displaystyle D^{(j)}{[L({\bm{p}})]}=e^{-\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\,,

(10a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{[L({\bm{p}})]}=e^{+\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\,.

(10b)

These boosts are obtained from the Wigner
D
-matrices of Eq. (
8
) by analytic continuation
[
37
]
.
L(\bm{p})
is the canonical standard boost
2
2

2

As they are connected to the identity,
L(\bm{p})
are elements of SO(3,1) and denote proper orthochronous Lorentz transformations
that transforms the four-momentum of a massive particle with mass
m
from its rest frame momentum
\overset{\circ}{p}{\vphantom{p}}^{\mu}
to the on-shell four-momentum
p^{\mu}
:

\displaystyle p^{\mu}=\mathchoice{L(\bm{p})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{L(\bm{p})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{L(\bm{p})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{L(\bm{p})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\,\overset{\circ}{p}{\vphantom{p}}^{\nu}\,,

(11)

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{p}{\vphantom{p}}^{\mu}\equiv(m,\bm{0})\,,

\displaystyle p^{\mu}=(E_{p},\bm{p})\equiv(\sqrt{m^{2}+\bm{p}^{2}},\bm{p})\,.

(12)

The boost parameters
\rho
(rapidity) and
\hat{\bm{p}}
(boost direction) are defined by

\displaystyle\sinh\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{|\bm{p}|}{m}\,,

(13a)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{p}}{|\bm{p}|}\,,

(13b)

and we have
\cosh\rho=\frac{E_{p}}{m}
.

For the chiral representations of Eq. (
7
), the generators of rotations are Hermitian and those of pure boosts are skew-Hermitian. As a consequence, rotation matrices are unitary,
and the boost matrices in Eqs. (
10
) are Hermitian.
Equation (
10
) shows that the two chiral representations for pure boosts are related to each other by

\displaystyle D^{(j)}[L^{-1}({\bm{p}})]=D^{(j)}[L(-\bm{p})]=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]\,.

(14)

For general Lorentz transformations
\Lambda
(combinations of boosts and rotations), the two chiral representations are related as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]=\left(D^{(j)}[\Lambda^{-1}]\right)^{\dagger}\,.

(15)

For the general linear group one can consider four independent representations on
d
-dimensional (complex) vector spaces. Starting with the defining or fundamental representation on a vector space
V
, we also have those on the complex conjugate
V^{*}
, the dual
\widetilde{V}
, and the dual complex conjugate
\widetilde{V}^{*}
vector spaces. When considering a subgroup of the general linear group, constraints that apply to the elements of the subgroup can result in
isomorphisms between two (or more) of the four representations above, which reduces the number of independent representations. For SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
), the fundamental representation has 2 by 2 complex matrices with unit determinant. The unit determinant requirement implies the presence of an invariant rank-2 tensor,
3
3

3

We always use the word tensor in relation to Lorentz transformations. It can refer either to vector or chiral representations, which can always be inferred by context.

the two-dimensional Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon^{ij}
. For all chiral representations of higher spin, this invariant tensor can also be introduced and is denoted as
C
here. Its properties are detailed in the next paragraph. The existence of
C
leads to the equivalence of the representations
V
and its dual
\widetilde{V}
. Similarly,
V^{*}
and its dual
\widetilde{V}^{*}
are also equivalent. In analogy to the spin-1/2 case
[
38
]
,
C
can be used to raise and lower indices on spinors. We follow the standard spinor index notation,
4
4

4

See App.
A
for conventions regarding index notations

\displaystyle\psi_{c}\in V\,,

(16a)

\displaystyle\psi^{c}\equiv\mathchoice{C^{{{cd}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67088pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33633pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{cd}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67088pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33633pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{cd}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67088pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33633pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{cd}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.73662pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67088pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33633pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\psi_{d}\in\widetilde{V}\,,

(16b)

\displaystyle\chi^{*\dot{c}}\in\widetilde{V}^{*}\,,

(16c)

\displaystyle\chi^{*}_{\dot{c}}\equiv\mathchoice{(C^{-1})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\dot{c}\dot{d}}}}}{(C^{-1})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\dot{c}\dot{d}}}}}{(C^{-1})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\dot{c}\dot{d}}}}}{(C^{-1})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\dot{c}\dot{d}}}}}\chi^{*\dot{d}}\in V^{*}\,,

(16d)

where
C^{cd}=C^{\dot{c}\dot{d}}
.
The relation between the left and right chiral representations presented in Eq. (
15
) shows we can identify the left chiral spinors with
\psi_{c}
and the right chiral spinors with
\chi^{*\dot{c}}
. In the remainder of this section we keep the spinorial indices explicit in many expressions as it helps to show what representations certain objects belong to, as well as identifying invariants. In later sections we will often drop these indices for readability. From the context, the indices can always be restored if desired.

The defining equation for the invariant tensor
C
is

\quad C\bm{J}^{(j)}C^{-1}=-\bm{J}^{(j)*}=-\bm{J}^{(j)T}\,,

(17)

where we used the hermiticity of the
\bm{J}^{(j)}
in the last step.

C
is unitary and satisfies

\displaystyle CC^{\dagger}=C^{\dagger}C=\mathbb{1}^{(j)},

\displaystyle CC^{*}=(-)^{2j}\mathbb{1}^{(j)}\,.

(18)

The matrix
C
can be stated up to a phase factor which is conventionally chosen such that

\displaystyle C^{cd}=(-)^{j-c}\delta_{-c,d}\,,

(19)

\displaystyle C^{\dagger}={C}^{-1}=C^{T}=(-)^{2j}C\,,

(20)

where indices run from
-j,\ldots,j
.
Note that in this convention the matrix
C
is real. The only non-zero elements are
\pm 1
that alternate sign along the anti-diagonal, starting with
+1
for the top right element.
Using Eq. (
17
), the equivalence of representations can now be shown as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern {$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.47678pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.91199pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{fd}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}\sim V\,,

(21a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left(D^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}_{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}_{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}_{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)*}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}_{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathchoice{C^{{{-1}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyl 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{{\dot{e}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{f}}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{{\dot{e}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{f}}}}}\mathchoice{C^{{{\dot{f}\dot{d}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{\dot{f}\dot{d}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{\dot{f}\dot{d}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{C^{{{\dot{f}\dot{d}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle V^{*}\sim\widetilde{V}^{*}\,.

(21b)

from where, using Eq. (
15
), it follows that
C
is invariant under Lorentz transformations.

The chiral boosts of Eq. (
14
) play a central role in the construction of the causal fields by providing an intertwining map between the fields and the creation and annihilation operators
[
39
]
. The creation and annihilation operators form an infinite-dimensional unitary representation of the Lorentz group.
For the annihilation operator
a(\bm{p},j\lambda)
, labeled with momentum
\bm{p}
and SU(2) little group spin quantum number
\lambda
:

U(\Lambda)\,a(\bm{p},j\lambda)\,U^{-1}(\Lambda)=D_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{(j)}[R^{-1}_{\text{w}}(\bm{p},\Lambda)]\,a(\Lambda\bm{p},j\lambda^{\prime})\,,

(22)

where the
momentum-dependent
Wigner rotation (little group transformation) takes the form

R_{\text{w}}(\bm{p},\Lambda)\equiv L^{-1}(\Lambda\bm{p})\,\Lambda\,L(\bm{p})\,.

(23)

The creation/annihilation operators and single particle states have the following normalizations

\displaystyle[a(\bm{p},j\lambda),a^{\dagger}(\bm{p}^{\prime},j^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime})]_{\pm}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\delta(\bm{p}-\bm{p}^{\prime})\,,

(24a)

\displaystyle|p,j\lambda\rangle=\sqrt{2E_{p}}\,a^{\dagger}(\bm{p},j\lambda)|0\rangle\,,

(24b)

\displaystyle\langle p^{\prime},j^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime}|p,j\lambda\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}(2E_{p})\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\delta(\bm{p}-\bm{p}^{\prime})\,.

(24c)

By multiplying the creation/annihilation operators with the representation of the standard boost included in the Wigner rotation of Eq. (
23
), the
(2j+1)
-component causal fields
\varphi
(left-chiral) and
\chi^{*}
(right-chiral) transform
in their spinor index with the finite dimensional (but non-unitary) representations
D[\Lambda]
or
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[\Lambda]
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{c}(x)=\frac{m^{j}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{\text{d}^{3}\bm{p}}{(2E_{p})^{1/2}}

Here, we leave the spin-
j
index on the fields implicit,
a(p,\lambda)
is the particle annihilation operator, and
b^{\dagger}(p,\lambda)
the antiparticle creation operator. Contrary to the transformations of the creation/annihilation operators, where the Wigner rotations depend on the argument
\bm{p}
of the operators, the transformations on the spinor indices of the fields carry no dependence on the field coordinate
x
and are completely determined by the transformation
\Lambda
.

We can now identify the coefficients of the creation/annihilation operators as the chiral spinors
u_{L},u_{R}
:

(27b)

where we introduce the rest frame spinor

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{u}_{{L},c}(\lambda)=\overset{\circ}{u}_{R}{\vphantom{u}}^{\dot{c}}(\lambda)=m^{j}\delta_{c}^{\lambda}=m^{j}\delta^{\dot{c}\lambda}\,,

(28a)

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{\phi}(\lambda)\equiv\overset{\circ}{u}_{L}(\lambda)=\overset{\circ}{u}{\vphantom{u}}_{R}(\lambda)\,,

(28b)

with the notation of the last equation explicitly stating that in the rest frame there is no distinction between the two chiral spinors. The choice to introduce the Kronecker delta function with these indices is guided by the connection with the massive spinor-helicity formalism, which is detailed in Sec.
IV
. There, the position of the SU(2) little group polarization index
\lambda
plays a role and matches with the choice of upper indices in these expressions.
Ultimately, the correspondence between the three labels in Eq. (
28
), namely the two Lorentz spinor indices (
c
,
\dot{c}
) and the little group index (
\lambda
), is feasible because they all belong to the same SU(2) representation.

Up to the normalization factor
m^{j}
, these chiral spinors correspond to the columns of the matrices for the chiral standard boosts of Eq. (
10
). The chiral spinors have a
(2j+1)
-valued spinor index
c
, and
\lambda
(also (
2j+1
)-valued) is its little group spin projection quantum number. Similar to any wave function appearing in a field
5
5

5

This includes the well known Dirac spinor and polarization four-vectors for massive spin 1 particles.
, due to carrying both Lorentz and little group indices, the chiral spinors have the following transformation property

\displaystyle\mathchoice{D^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}u_{{L},d}(p,\lambda)=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}u_{{L},c}(\Lambda p,\lambda^{\prime})D^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}[R_{\text{w}}(\bm{p},\Lambda)]\,,

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[\Lambda]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{d}}}}}u_{R}^{\dot{d}}(p,\lambda)=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}u_{R}^{\dot{c}}(\Lambda p,\lambda^{\prime})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}[R_{\text{w}}(\bm{p},\Lambda)]\,.

(29b)

This can be inferred from writing the spinor as the overlap

\displaystyle\langle 0|\varphi_{c}(0)|\bm{p},\lambda\rangle=u_{{L},c}(p,\lambda)\,,

(30)

and apply a Lorentz boost to the field, see Eqs. (
26a
) and (
22
).

Other essential objects in Weinberg’s construction are the matrices
\Pi^{(j)}(p)
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}(p)
, which encode the outer product of the chiral spinors, and correspond to the numerator of propagators in Weinberg’s formalism
[
13
]
. They appear in the commutators of the chiral fields as

\displaystyle[\varphi_{c}(x),\varphi^{*}_{\dot{d}}(y)]_{\pm}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{\text{d}^{3}\bm{p}}{2E_{p}}\,\Pi_{c\dot{d}}(p)\left[e^{-{\rm i}p(x-y)}\pm e^{{\rm i}p(x-y)}\right]\,,

(31a)

\displaystyle[\chi^{*\dot{c}}(x),\chi^{d}(y)]_{\pm}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{\text{d}^{3}\bm{p}}{2E_{p}}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\dot{c}d}(p)\left[e^{-{\rm i}p(x-y)}\pm e^{{\rm i}p(x-y)}\right]\,,

(31b)

where the
\pm
in the left-hand side denotes the choice of sign for fermions and bosons and we left the spin index
j
on the
\Pi
implicit.

From Eqs. (
25
) and (
24a
), it can be inferred that these matrices take the form

\displaystyle\Pi_{c\dot{d}}(p)=m^{2j}\mathchoice{D^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\delta_{e\dot{e}}\mathchoice{D^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}_{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}_{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}_{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{D^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}_{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=m^{2j}\left(e^{-2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)_{cd}\,,

(32a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\dot{c}d}(p)=m^{2j}\mathchoice{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{\dot{c}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\dot{e}}}}}\delta^{\dot{e}e}\mathchoice{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)*}[L(\bm{p})]^{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}}=m^{2j}\left(e^{2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)_{cd}\,.

(32b)

The position of the
e,\dot{e}
indices warrants a comment, as we are summing over two upper indices, which does not seem to be an invariant operation. However, both those indices refer to rest frame spinor indices which can be identified to the little group polarization indices (see Eq. (
27
)), where the difference between the representations disappears (see Eq. (
28
)). On the right-hand side, the
\bm{J}^{(j)}
do not carry information about any of the representations considered in Eq. (
16
), so we opted to not distinguish them between the two equations.
The crux of Weinberg’s construction is that the
\Pi^{(j)}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
can equivalently be written as a symmetric and traceless rank-
2j
tensor in space-time indices (denoted as
t
-tensors from here on) contracted with
2j
copies of the four-momentum
p^{\mu}
. We will not repeat the proof here (see Ref.
[
13
]
) and only state the final result:

\displaystyle m^{2j}\left(e^{-2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)_{cd}=\Pi_{c\dot{d}}(p)=\mathchoice{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.89828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29242pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{c\dot{d}}}}}{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.89828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29242pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{c\dot{d}}}}}{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.89828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29242pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{c\dot{d}}}}}{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35153pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.89828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29242pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{c\dot{d}}}}}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}\,,

(33a)

\displaystyle m^{2j}\left(e^{2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)_{cd}=\mathchoice{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(p)=\mathchoice{\left(\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{{\dot{c}d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94066pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.32817pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}\,.

(33b)

We want to stress that these
t
-tensors have both Lorentz and spinor indices, meaning each element of the tensor with specific Lorentz indices is a square matrix of order
2j+1
.
The
t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}
and
\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}
are
the generalization to arbitrary spin of the so-called Pauli four-vector or Infeld-Van der Waerden symbols
6
6

6

We thank Cédric Lorcé for making us aware of the latter terminology.

\sigma^{\mu}\equiv t^{\mu}=(\mathbb{1}^{(2)},\bm{\sigma})
and
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\equiv\bar{t}^{\mu}=(\mathbb{1}^{(2)},-\bm{\sigma})
formed with the Pauli matrices
\bm{\sigma}

[
40
]
.

The
t
-tensors have the following properties:

1.

The matrices in the
t
-tensors are Hermitian. This can be seen by taking the complex conjugated transpose of Eq. (
33
).
Since the generators of rotations
\bm{J}^{(j)}
are Hermitian, and the parameters in the exponential [Eq. (
13
)] are real, then the left-hand side of the equation remains unchanged. On the right-hand side the momenta are real, thus we have

\displaystyle\left(t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{\dagger}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}=\;

\displaystyle m^{2j}\left(e^{-2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)^{\dagger}=m^{2j}e^{-2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle t^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}\,,

(34a)

\displaystyle\left(\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{\dagger}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}=\;

\displaystyle m^{2j}\left(e^{2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}\right)^{\dagger}=m^{2j}e^{2\rho\,\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}\cdots p_{\mu_{2j}}\,.

(34b)

2.

They are symmetric under any permutation
\pi
of the space-time indices, and are Lorentz traceless in any pair of indices:

\displaystyle t_{c\dot{d}}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{2j}}=t_{c\dot{d}}^{\mu_{\pi(1)}\mu_{\pi(2)}\cdots\mu_{\pi(2j)}}\,,

(35)

\displaystyle g_{\mu_{k}\mu_{l}}t_{c\dot{d}}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{k}\cdots\mu_{l}\cdots\mu_{2j}}=0\,.

(36)

3.

Evaluating Eqs. (
33
) with
\rho=0
[see Eq. (
13
)], one obtains that the
t
-tensors always contain the identity as the element with all zero indices:

t^{0\cdots 0}=\bar{t}^{0\cdots 0}=\mathbb{1}^{(j)}\,.

(37)

4.

They act as intertwiners
[
41
]
between the SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
) spinor outer product representation

(0,j)^{*}\otimes(0,j)\sim(j,0)\otimes(0,j)\sim(j,j)\,,

(38)

and symmetric traceless Lorentz tensors of rank
2j
. Consequently, they exhibit the following transformation properties under Lorentz transformations:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left(D^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[\Lambda]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.05936pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.40747pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{e\dot{f}}}}}{\left(t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox (39a)

(39b)

The covariant transformation property of the
t
-tensors can be contrasted with that of the individual spinor of Eq. (
29
), where SU(2) little group transformations appear through the momentum-dependent Wigner rotation.
In the outer product of Eq. (
31
), little group indices are contracted and these Wigner rotations cancel out, which leads to the invariance of the
\Pi(p)
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(p)
, leading in turn to Eq. (
39
).

5.

If we introduce the parity conjugated four momentum
\bar{p}^{\mu}\equiv(E_{p},-\bm{p})
, from Eq. (
32
)
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}(p)=\Pi^{(j)}(\bar{p})
follows. Using Eq. (
33
), one finds by identifying equal powers of
\bm{J}^{(j)}
that elements of
t
and
\bar{t}
are related by

\displaystyle\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\ldots\mu_{2j}}=t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\ldots\mu_{2j}}\,.

(40)

Using the matrix
C
to relate left and right chiral boosts, this can be formulated covariantly as

\displaystyle\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}=C\left(t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{*}C^{-1}\,.

(41)

We will refer to Eq. (
41
) as the barring operation. Extending the barring operation to include general expressions is straightforward. In general, we have

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\left(\cdots\right)_{1}\,{t}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\,\left(\cdots\right)_{2}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=C\left[\left(\cdots\right)_{1}\,t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\,\left(\cdots\right)_{2}\right]^{*}C^{-1}

\displaystyle=C(\cdots)_{1}^{*}C^{-1}\,C\left(t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\right)^{*}C^{-1}\,C(\cdots)_{2}^{*}C^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\cdots)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\,\bar{t}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\cdots)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}\,.

(42)

If the expression inside parenthesis does not contains
t
-tensors, the barring operation reduces to complex conjugation. Explicitly

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\cdots)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{no $t$-tensor}}=C(\cdots)_{\text{no matrices}}^{*}C^{-1}=(\cdots)^{*}\,.

(43)

6.

Chiral fields for larger spin values can be built out of tensor products of lower spin chiral fields applying SU(2) tensor product reductions using Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. This leads to the following recursion relations that the
t
-tensors obey
[
12
,
14
]
