VI.2
) were obtained by writing the momentum dependent spinors as a boost, represented by a matrix in spinor space, acting on a rest-frame spinor. Then the combination of boost and Dirac matrices are simplified by using the reduction formulas for the
t
-tensors. Up to here the formulas are valid for both “Full
\tilde{p}
” and “Canonical-Melosh” approaches.

In the “Full
\tilde{p}
” approach, all the information about the motion of the particles appears in the boost parameters
\tilde{p}
, which are spinor choice dependent and in general complex valued. The rest-frame spinors are the simplest possible [Eq. (
66
)] and select the element on row
\lambda_{f}
and column
\lambda_{i}
in the
t
-tensor matrices. From Sec.
III
, we know that in the light-front spherical basis these matrices only contain one non-zero element and are thus extremely sparse, simplifying the numerical evaluation.

In the “Canonical-Melosh” approach, the motion of the particles is contained both in the rest-frame spinors
\langle\lambda_{f}|\cdots|\lambda_{i}\rangle
, which are now the
Melosh-rotated
spinors of Eq. (
256
), and also in the boost parameters
\tilde{p}=\tilde{p}_{c}
, which are real valued.
The latter property allows to further simplify the expressions. The (pseudo)tensor bilinear can be written as

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})\,\gamma^{{\mu}_{1}\cdots{\mu}_{2j}}\,u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\operatorname{Re}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t+\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t-\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right\}\,,

(210a)

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})\,\gamma^{{\mu}_{1}\cdots{\mu}_{2j}}\gamma_{5}\,u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=-m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\operatorname{Re}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t-\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t+\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right\}\,,

(210b)

\displaystyle[\text{Canonical-Melosh}]

where we wrote
\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|
,
|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle
to emphasize the Melosh-rotated nature of these rest-frame spinors, and include a subindex
c
to remind us that canonical boost parameters must be used.
From these expressions, it is straightforward to read off the behavior of these bilinears under parity.
\operatorname{Re}[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
(
\operatorname{Im}[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{V}^{\mu_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
) contains an even (odd) number of Levi-Civita tensors, thus behaves as a tensor (pseudo-tensor) under parity. Meanwhile, parity switches the positions of the left- and right-chiral spinor in the bispinor, which in Eqs. (
210
) results in interchanging the
t
and
\bar{t}
matrix element.
Therefore, bilinear (
210a
) transforms as a proper tensor. For bilinear (
210b
) there is a sign change, thus it transforms as a pseudotensor.
Note that Eq. (
40
) also restricts the number of spatial indices on the elements of the
t
-tensors that contribute in each of the terms, i.e. even (odd) in the first (second) term of Eq. (
210a
) and vice versa for the pseudotensor epression. These agree with the discussed behavior under parity.

It is worth noting that both approaches have their advantages in evaluating these bilinears.
For every type of spinor the
Canonical-Melosh
has no difference in the Lorentz-tensor structure, which is entirely written using invariant tensors and the same (real) canonical parameters
\widetilde{P},\widetilde{\Delta}
. The difference between spinors type appears in the
t
-matrix elements, which are evaluated between the Melosh-rotated rest-frame spinors. The Melosh rotation, although straightforward to calculate, depends on the momentum of the particles in the bilinear.
In the
Full
\tilde{p}
approach Eqs. (
206
), (
VI.2
) are used.
For those expressions, the
t
-matrix elements remain the same for any spin types. Equation (
90
) can be used to identify which specific Lorentz components of the
t
-tensors contribute to a given spin transition.
These components, combined with
\widetilde{P}
and
\widetilde{\Delta}
, determine the non-zero matrix elements of the bilinear.
In both approaches one can take advantage of the
\{+-R\,L\}
-basis for the contraction in Lorentz indices, as it was shown in Sec.
III
that the
t
-tensors are particularly simple and sparse in that basis.

VI.3
Scalar and pseudo-scalar bilinears

The scalar bilinear corresponds to the special case
m=0
of the covariantly independent bi-indexed elements
\mathsf{G}_{m}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{m}\mu_{m}}
, which we present in the next section.
We single it out here, however, because it is the simplest multipole (monopole) and it is present for all spin representations.
Substituting
\Gamma=\mathbb{1}
in Eq. (
VI.1
), we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}{(p_{f},s_{f})}u{(p_{i},s_{i})}

\displaystyle=\overset{\circ}{u}^{\dagger}(\lambda_{f})\mathcal{P}^{f}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{2j}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&{t}^{(\beta)}\bar{t}^{(\alpha)}\\
\bar{t}^{(\beta)}{t}^{(\alpha)}&0\end{array}\right)\mathcal{P}^{i}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2j}}\overset{\circ}{u}(\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=\overset{\circ}{\phi}^{\dagger}(\lambda_{f})\left[\left(\prod^{2j}_{l=1}\tilde{p}_{f,{\beta}_{l}}\,\tilde{p}_{i,{\alpha}_{l}}^{*}\right){t}^{(\beta)}\bar{t}^{(\alpha)}+\left(\prod^{2j}_{l=1}\tilde{p}_{f,{\beta}_{l}}^{*}\,\tilde{p}_{i,{\alpha}_{l}}\right)\bar{t}^{(\beta)}{t}^{(\alpha)}\right]\overset{\circ}{\phi}(\lambda_{i})\,,

(213)

where rest frame chiral spinors
\overset{\circ}{\phi}(\lambda)
are given in Eqs. (
28
).
Making use of the reduction formulae for the quadratic products of
t
-tensors
Eqs. (
110
), and the notation of Eq. (
202
), we find

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\xi_{l}}\right)\langle\lambda_{f}|t_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle+\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\xi_{l}}\right)^{*}\langle\lambda_{f}|\bar{t}_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle\right\}\,.

(214)

For the pseudo-scalar
\Gamma=\gamma_{5}
in Eq. (
VI.1
), we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}{(p_{f},s_{f})}\gamma_{5}u{(p_{i},s_{i})}

\displaystyle=\overset{\circ}{u}^{\dagger}(\lambda_{f})\mathcal{P}^{f}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{2j}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&{t}^{(\beta)}\bar{t}^{(\alpha)}\\
-\bar{t}^{(\beta)}{t}^{(\alpha)}&0\end{array}\right)\mathcal{P}^{i}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2j}}\overset{\circ}{u}(\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\xi_{l}}\right)\langle\lambda_{f}|t_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle-\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\xi_{l}}\right)^{*}\langle\lambda_{f}|\bar{t}_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle\right\},

(217)

where the only difference with the scalar bilinear of Eq. (
VI.3
) is the sign of the second term, originating from the extra
\gamma_{5}
.

We take advantage of the “Canonical-Melosh” approach, where the (canonical) boost-parameters are real valued, to simplify further.
Using Eqs. (
204
) we introduce

\mathsf{S}^{\xi}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})=\mathcal{Q}^{\tilde{p}_{f}^{c}\tilde{p}_{i}^{c}\xi}=\left(\widetilde{P}_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\widetilde{\Delta}_{c}^{2}\right)\eta^{\xi}-\left(\widetilde{P}_{c}^{0}\widetilde{\Delta}_{c}^{\xi}-\widetilde{\Delta}_{c}^{0}\widetilde{P}_{c}^{\xi}\right)+{\rm i}\varepsilon^{0\widetilde{\Delta}_{c}\widetilde{P}_{c}\xi}\,,

(218)

where the first term on the right side is zero when evaluated in the Breit frame (see remark in the previous section).
The (pseudo)scalar bilinear can be written as

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\operatorname{Re}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t+\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 56.9055pt+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t-\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right\}\,,

(219a)

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})\,\gamma_{5}\,u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\operatorname{Re}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t-\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 56.9055pt+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t+\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right\}\,,

(219b)

\displaystyle[\text{Canonical-Melosh}]

where we wrote
\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|\cdots|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle
to emphasize the Melosh-rotated nature of these rest-frame spinors, and include a subindex
c
to emphasize that (real-valued) canonical boost parameters must be used. From these expressions, it is straightforward to read off the behavior of these bilinears under parity.

\operatorname{Re}[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
(
\operatorname{Im}[\prod_{l=1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
) contains an even (odd) number of Levi-Civita tensors, thus behaves as a proper tensor (pseudo-tensor) under parity. Meanwhile, parity switches the positions of the left- and right-chiral spinor in the bispinor, which results in interchanging the
t
and
\bar{t}
matrix element.
For bilinear (
219a
) the expression does not change sign under a parity inversion, meaning it transforms as a scalar. For bilinear (
219b
) there is a sign change, thus it transforms as a pseudo-scalar.

VI.4
Bilinears of bi-indexed tensors

For the sake of simplicity of the resulting expressions, in this section we provide a general expression for bi-indexed-tensor bilinears in terms of the Dirac basis elements
\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}
instead of the multipoles
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}
. The bilinears in terms of the covariant multipoles can be obtained using the equations (
172
) and (
147
) together with the results shown here.

Each of the
2j
covariantly independent bi-indexed-tensor bilinears related to
\Gamma=\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}
(
1\leq m\leq 2j
) corresponds to

\displaystyle\bar{u}{(p_{f},s_{f})}\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}u{(p_{i},s_{i})}=

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{\phi}^{\dagger}(\lambda_{f})\left[\left(\prod^{2j}_{l=1}\tilde{p}_{f,{\beta}_{l}}\,\tilde{p}_{i,{\alpha}_{l}}^{*}\right){t}^{(\beta)}\,\bar{t}_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{m}0\cdots 0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}\right)\bar{t}^{(\alpha)}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left.\;+\left(\prod^{2j}_{l=1}\tilde{p}_{f,{\beta}_{l}}^{*}\,\tilde{p}_{i,{\alpha}_{l}}\right)\bar{t}^{(\beta)}\,t_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{m}0\cdots 0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\text{red}}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}\right){t}^{(\alpha)}\right]\overset{\circ}{\phi}(\lambda_{i})\,,

(220)

where rest-frame chiral spinors
\overset{\circ}{\phi}(\lambda)
are given in Eqs. (
28
).
In order to simplify Eq. (
220
) we use twice the reduction formulas for the quadratic products of
t
-tensors Eqs. (
110
). As suggested by the grouping in Eq. (
220
), we perform a first reduction on the quadratic product
{t}^{(\beta)}\,\bar{t}_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{m}0\cdots 0}
(
\bar{t}^{(\beta)}\,{t}_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{m}0\cdots 0}
), see Sec.
V.2.2
for details on a method of reducing these products. The resulting
t
-tensor (
\bar{t}
-tensor) is combined with the remaining
\bar{t}^{(\alpha)}
(
{t}^{(\alpha)}
) and subsequently also reduced.
We find

\displaystyle\bar{u}{(p_{f},s_{f})}\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}u{(p_{i},s_{i})}=

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}}{}_{\xi_{l}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\xi_{l}}{}_{\sigma_{l}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\sigma_{l}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\nu_{l}}\right]\left[\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\nu_{l}}\right]\langle\lambda_{f}|t_{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle+\left[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}}{}_{\xi_{l}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\xi_{l}}{}_{\sigma_{l}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\sigma_{l}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\nu_{l}}\right]^{*}\left[\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}\tilde{p}_{i}^{*}\nu_{l}}\right]^{*}\langle\lambda_{f}|\bar{t}_{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{2j}}|\lambda_{i}\rangle\,.

(221)

In the “Canonical-Melosh” approach, using Eqs. (
204
) and the fact that the canonical boost parameters are real valued, we introduce

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}^{\mu\rho\nu}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})=

\displaystyle\;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\,\mu\rho}{\,}{}_{\xi}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}^{c}\xi}{}_{\sigma}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\sigma\tilde{p}_{i}^{c}{}^{*}\nu}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\,\mu\rho}{\,}{}_{\xi}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\tilde{p}_{f}^{c}\xi}{}_{\sigma}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\sigma\tilde{p}_{i}^{c}\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2\left(\tilde{P}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{P}^{\nu}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\nu}_{c}\right)\eta^{\rho}-2\left(\tilde{P}^{\rho}_{c}\tilde{P}^{\nu}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{\rho}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\nu}_{c}\right)\eta^{\mu}

\displaystyle+2\left(\tilde{P}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{0}_{c}\right)g^{\rho\nu}-2\left(\tilde{P}^{\rho}_{c}\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{\rho}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{0}_{c}\right)g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle+\left(\tilde{P}^{2}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{2}_{c}\right)\left(\eta^{\mu}g^{\rho\nu}-\eta^{\rho}g^{\mu\nu}\right)+\left(\tilde{P}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\rho}_{c}-\tilde{P}^{\rho}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\mu}_{c}\right)\eta^{\nu}

\displaystyle-2\left(\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\rho}_{c}-\tilde{P}^{\rho}\tilde{\Delta}^{0}\right)g^{\mu\nu}+2\left(\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{\mu}-\tilde{P}^{\mu}_{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{0}_{c}\right)g^{\rho\nu}

\displaystyle+{\rm i}{\epsilon}^{\tilde{\Delta}_{c}\tilde{P}_{c}\rho\nu}\eta^{\mu}-{\rm i}{\epsilon}^{\tilde{\Delta}_{c}\tilde{P}_{c}\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho}+{\rm i}{\epsilon}^{0\tilde{P}_{c}\mu\rho}\tilde{P}^{\nu}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}{\rm i}{\epsilon}^{0\tilde{\Delta}_{c}\mu\rho}\tilde{\Delta}^{\nu}_{c}

\displaystyle+\left(\tilde{P}^{2}_{c}-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\Delta}^{2}_{c}\right){\rm i}\epsilon^{0\mu\rho\nu}+\left(2\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}-\tilde{\Delta}^{0}_{c}\right){\rm i}\epsilon^{\tilde{P}_{c}\mu\rho\nu}+\left(\tilde{P}^{0}_{c}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\Delta}^{0}_{c}\right){\rm i}\epsilon^{\tilde{\Delta}_{c}\mu\rho\nu}\,,

(222)

to write the bi-indexed bilinears as

\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{f},\lambda_{f})\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}u(p_{i},\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=m_{f}^{j}m_{i}^{j}\left\{\operatorname{Re}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mathsf{B}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t+\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\;+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mathsf{B}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\right]\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|(t-\bar{t})_{\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{2j}}|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle\right\}\,,

(223)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt[\text{Canonical-Melosh}]

where
\mathsf{S}^{\xi}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})
is given by Eq. (
218
). We wrote
\langle\tilde{\lambda}_{f}|\cdots|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\rangle
to emphasize the Melosh-rotated nature of these rest-frame spinors, and include a subindex
c
to remind us that (real-valued) canonical boost parameters must be used. For
m=0
Eq. (
219a
) is recovered. From these expressions, it is straightforward to read off the behavior of these bilinears under parity.
The term
\operatorname{Re}[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mathsf{B}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
contains an even number of Levi-Civita pseudotensors, while
\operatorname{Im}[\prod_{l=1}^{m}\mathsf{B}^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})\prod_{l=m+1}^{2j}\mathsf{S}^{\xi_{l}}(\widetilde{P}_{c},\widetilde{\Delta}_{c})]
contains an odd number of Levi-Civita pseudotensors, thus under parity they behave as a tensor and pseudo-tensor respectively.
Meanwhile, parity switches the positions of the left- and right-chiral spinor in the bispinor, which results in interchanging the
t
and
\bar{t}
matrix element.
Therefore, the bi-indexed bilinears behave as proper tensors under a parity inversion.

VII
On-shell identities

It is well-known in the spin-1/2 case that for the on-shell case, not all the bilinears considered in Sec.
VI
are independent. These are the famous Gordon identities
[
47
]
. As is shown here, the same is true for the general spin case. This allows to reduce (or choose) the number of independent bilinears that need to be considered in the decomposition of operator matrix elements.

We start from the generalized Dirac equation,

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}(p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}})-m^{2j}\right)u(p,\lambda)=\left({p\hskip-5.0pt/}^{(j)}-m^{2j}\right)u(p,\lambda)=0\,,

(224)

where,
{p\hskip-5.0pt/}^{(j)}=\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}}
.
The Dirac equation leads to generalized on-shell identities

\bar{u}(p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\left(\Gamma\right)u(p,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\bar{m}^{2j}}\bar{u}(p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\left(\left\{{P\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)},\Gamma\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\left[{\Delta\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)},\Gamma\right]\right)u(p,\lambda)\,,

(225)

and

0=\bar{u}(p^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\{{\Delta\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)},\Gamma\right\}+\left[{P\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)},\Gamma\right]\right)u(p,\lambda)\,.

(226)

Here,
\Gamma
stands for any of the independent elements of the generalized Dirac basis in Eq. (
179
):

\displaystyle\Gamma=\mathbb{1}:

\displaystyle\ \text{scalar},

\displaystyle\Gamma=\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}:

\displaystyle\ \text{symmetric traceless proper rank-$2j$ tensor},

\displaystyle\Gamma=\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{m}\mu_{m}}:

\displaystyle\ \text{bi-indexed traceless rank-$2m$ tensors}\ (1\leq m\leq 2j),

\displaystyle\Gamma=\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}\gamma_{5}:

\displaystyle\ \text{symmetric traceless rank-$2j$ pseudotensor},

\displaystyle\Gamma=\gamma_{5}:

\displaystyle\ \text{pseudoscalar},

we introduced the notation
{P\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)}=\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}P_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}
,
{\Delta\hskip-7.0pt/}^{(j)}=\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}\Delta_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}
, where

\displaystyle P_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{m}^{2j}}{2}\left(\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}}\dots p^{\prime}_{\mu_{2j}}}{{m^{\prime}}^{2j}}+\frac{p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}}}{m^{2j}}\right)\,,

(227)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}

\displaystyle=\bar{m}^{2j}\left(\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}}\dots p^{\prime}_{\mu_{2j}}}{{m^{\prime}}^{2j}}-\frac{p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}}}{m^{2j}}\right)\,,

(228)

\displaystyle\bar{m}^{2j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({m^{\prime}}^{2j}+m^{2j}\right)\,.

(229)

These statisfy the following symmetry and orthogonality properties:

\displaystyle P^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}{\left(p^{\prime},p\right)}=

\displaystyle\;P^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}{\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}\,,

(230)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}\left(p^{\prime},p\right)=

\displaystyle\;-\Delta^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)\,,

(231)

\displaystyle P^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}\Delta_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}=

\displaystyle\;0\,.

(232)

If the initial and final masses are equal
m^{\prime}=m
, the definitions of
P_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}
and
\Delta_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}
reduce to

\displaystyle\bar{m}^{2j}

\displaystyle=m^{2j}\,,

(233)

\displaystyle P_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(p^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}}\dots p^{\prime}_{\mu_{2j}}+p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}}\right)\,,

(234)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2j}}

\displaystyle=\left(p^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}}\dots p^{\prime}_{\mu_{2j}}-p_{\mu_{1}}\dots p_{\mu_{2j}}\right)\,.

(235)

The existence of on-shell identities
means that not all terms within the full set of bilinears are independent, and the identities can be used to reduce the set into an independent one. More specifically, the on-shell identities mean that we can always replace bilinears of
\gamma^{(\mu)},\gamma^{(\mu)}\gamma_{5}
with bilinears of commutators and anticommutators of two gamma matrices, which correspond to linear combinations of bilinears of
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(\mu\rho)}
. This implies one can always work with block-
diagonal
elements of the Dirac basis,
i.e.

\mathbb{1}\,,\,\gamma_{5}\,,\,\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{m}\mu_{m}}\,(1\leq m\leq 2j)
, which can be identified with specific multipoles.

VIII
Discussion and outlook

If we want to summarize the constructions presented here in one sentence, we would describe it as repackaging expressions with general SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
) chiral (bi)spinors into Lorentz tensors. Working with the Lorentz tensors simplifies the construction of covariant (or invariant) expressions. For the spin-1/2 case this is something that we all have learned when studying the Dirac calculus, here we exposed details and interconnections for the general higher-spin case. The
t
-tensors, and their derived objects (
\gamma
-matrices, the
\mathcal{T}_{m},\mathcal{G}_{m}
multipoles etc.), allow for the mapping between these different (repackaged) tensor representations of the Lorentz group’s double cover SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
). This property is reflected in the
t
-tensors and generalized Dirac basis carrying both spinorial (matrix) and Lorentz (tensor) indices. For general spin
j
, these objects contain a convenient packaging of all the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients needed to build the larger spin representations, but in such a way that they still transform covariantly. The mapping to Lorentz tensors facilitates the composition of invariants and allow us to circumvent spinor calculations, which can be less intuitive to deal with.

Our results in Sec.
VII
show that on-shell bilinears for any spin-
j
can be parametrized exclusively using a complete set of covariant
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
multipoles. This is a very appealing result, which is valid for any spin.
Our final expressions allow simple switching between different types of spinors through a change in the
\tilde{p}
parameter or implementing Melosh rotations on the rest-frame spinors. The multipole structure allows immediate identification of similar structures across the different spin cases. The use of chiral (bi)spinors also means we are using objects containing only the physical degrees of freedom and no extra constraints need to be imposed. The algorithm to construct the
t
-tensors presented in Sec.
III
, in combination with the bilinear expressions presented in Sec.
VI
, demonstrate that the whole framework is straightforward to implement in calculations or numerical evaluations.

Of course, a framework only becomes practical when it can be applied to relevant applications. We want to use our results to have a fresh look at the matrix elements of QCD operators used in non-perturbative phenomenology. Local operators encode the form factors (electromagentic, weak, gravitational, generalized) of hadronic and nuclear bound states, while bilocal light-ray operators do the same for partonic distribution functions (collinear, transverse, generalized,
\ldots
). The use of multipole bilinears will aid to parametrize these operators in a unified and systematic manner for any spin. This should make comparisons between the different spin cases more straightforward. These studies will be the topic of follow-up work. The goal there will not necessarily be to replace existing parametrizations and decompositions, as any decomposition in the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
multipoles will result in distributions that are a linear combination of existing ones. Instead, our aim is to provide a more straightforward identification for the physical content of each distribution. The resulting expressions will apply to any spin, which is advantageous from both a theoretical and practical standpoint. Our expressions hold in any frame, but to make the connection with phenomenology, using preferential frames like the Breit or Drell-Yan frame will put to zero several of the parameters and yield simplified expressions.

Our study focused on matrix element with the same spin content in the initial and final state. A more general treatment of matrix elements for nuclear and hadronic amplitudes of interest would also include transition matrix element between particles of different spin, or different number of particles in the initial and final state (e.g. breakup matrix elements). The treatment of these cases should also be possible through extensions of the framework presented here. While not discussed in this work, the
t
-tensors exist for more general
(j,j^{\prime})
representations
[
12
,
13
]
, although Weinberg shows that for causal fields they can be written as derivatives of
(j-j^{\prime},0)
or
(0,j^{\prime}-j)
representations. These tensors (and derived objects from their algebra) can still, however, be useful in transitions between states of different spin. Additionally, Williams
[
14
]
has also introduced objects that intertwine between one whole-valued
(m,0)
or
(0,m)
spinorial representation and bi-indexed Lorentz tensors. With all these ingredients it is possible to build structures that are linear in the spinors of all involved particles and carry a definite Lorentz tensor structure. Separately, a dedicated study of the
(j,j^{\prime})
representations could still be interesting for its own sake.

The bilinears for which expressions were constructed still carry dependence on the little group polarization indices of the spinors. Consequently, they have combined Lorentz and little group covariance properties. These little group characteristics reflect themselves in the appearance of the

\tilde{p}^{\mu}
parameters in the final expressions. Observables (cross sections, asymmetries etc.) do not have this explicit little group dependence anymore because the
S
-matrix amplitude and its complex conjugate are contracted in their little group indices with the density matrices of initial and final state particles, which also carry little group indices. Explicit appearance of little group indices can be avoided now, by attaching the objects that carry the little group indices in the
S
-matrix amplitude (spinors, polarization vectors, etc.) to the density matrix and as such introduce a covariant density matrix and covariant “amputated” amplitudes that only carry Lorentz indices, see Ref.
[
48
]
. To quote the well-known spin-1/2 example for the covariant spin-density matrix:

\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\rho(\bm{S})_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\,u(p,\lambda)\bar{u}(p,\lambda^{\prime})=(\not{p}+m)\left(\frac{1+\gamma_{5}\not{S}}{2}\right),

(236)

where
\bm{S}
is the polarization vector characterizing the rest-frame spin-1/2 ensemble, and
S^{\mu}=\mathchoice{\left(\Lambda_{\text{SB}}[p]\right)^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\left(\Lambda_{\text{SB}}[p]\right)^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\left(\Lambda_{\text{SB}}[p]\right)^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\left(\Lambda_{\text{SB}}[p]\right)^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\overset{\circ}{S}^{\nu}\;[\overset{\circ}{S}^{\mu}=(0,\bm{S})]
its covariant generalization. Therefore, to accommodate polarized targets or beams in this formalism, it could be interesting to analyze the covariant density matrices for these chiral spinors too, building on the earlier work by Zwanziger
[
48
]
.

As outlined in Sec.
IV
, we note that the
(j,0)\oplus(0,j)
spinor construction used in this work can be mapped to the massive spinor-helicity formalism of Ref.
[
18
]
.
This connection opens the door to applying our covariant multipole decomposition directly within modern on-shell amplitude computations, bridging non-perturbative QCD applications and high-energy scattering methods.
Finally, all our results were derived
using the 1+3D flat space-time geometry of the Standard Model. We did not consider any
other cases here, but extensions in different dimensions and/or non-flat geometries could
be an interesting topic of study.

Appendix A
Conventions and notation

Throughout this article, we use the following standard conventions:

•

The index conventions are

1.

Lowercase Greek letters for space-time Lorentz four-vector indices.

2.

Lowercase Roman letters at the start of the alphabet (
a,b,\ldots,f
) for SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
) left-chiral spinorial indices. For the right-chiral representation we use the same convention with dotted indices. For spin-
j
representations, these indices take values from
-j
to
j
. In most of the text, we reserve the use of
a,b
for the spin-1/2 case, and use
c,d,\ldots
for the general spin case. The exception is App.
D
, see specific comments there.

3.

We use
\lambda
for the SU(2) little group polarization index of the chiral spinors. In Sec.
IV
, we use uppercase Roman letters
A,B
for these indices to make the connection with the MSH notation more immediate.

4.

We use lowercase Roman letters in the middle of the alphabet
i,j,\ldots,n,r,s
as general counters in summations and products or as Lorentz indices in expressions where only spatial indices should be considered. The complex number
\mathrm{i}^{2}=-1
is distinguished from the index
i
.

5.

In Sec.
V
, we use
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}
to indicate the number of plus, minus, left and right indices in the
t
-tensor matrix elements.

6.

For bi-spinors we do not write any spinor index explicitly, as they contain spinors from both chiralities. We denote with
u(p,\lambda)
the canonical bi-spinor (no label). For the helicity spinors we use
u_{h}(p,\lambda)
,
u_{h^{\prime}}(p,\lambda)
, and for light-front
u_{LF}(p,\lambda)
.

•

The Minkowski metric is

g^{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\,,

(237)

and the covariant Levi-Civita tensor has sign
\epsilon^{0123}=1
.

•

The relation between a general element
g
of the Lie group
G
and the generators of the group, which form a basis
X_{i}
for the associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, is given by the exponential map

g=e^{-{\rm i}\sum_{i}t_{i}X_{i}}\,.

(238)

The real parameters
t_{i}
uniquely characterize the group element and we always use a minus sign in the exponential map (both for rotations and boosts).

•

The construction uses left- and right-handed chiral spinors (defined in Sec.
II.1
), and bispinors as parity conserving direct sum representations of a left- and right-handed spinor. For the bispinors we work in the Weyl representation, with the left-handed (right-handed) spinor occupies the top (bottom) rows of the bispinor, see Sec.
II.4
.

•

For any representation of spin
j
,
\gamma_{5}
is defined in the Weyl representation as

\gamma_{5}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathbb{1}^{(j)}&0\\
0&\mathbb{1}^{(j)}\end{array}\right)\,,

(239)

where
\mathbb{1}^{(j)}
is the identity matrix of rank
2j+1
. For spin 1/2, this corresponds to

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}={\rm i}\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}\,,

(240)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}[\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\rho}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma_{5}]=-4{\rm i}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,.

(241)

•

We use light-front and transverse spherical components

\displaystyle a^{\pm}=a^{0}\pm a^{3}\,,

\displaystyle a^{R}=a^{1}+{\rm i}a^{2}\,,

\displaystyle a^{L}=a^{1}-{\rm i}a^{2}\,.

(242)

•

The convention used for the covariant Lorentz generators
\mathbb{M}^{\mu\nu}
(
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mu\nu}
) is shown in Eqs. (
120
).

•

The binomial coefficient symbol is given by

\binom{2j}{m}=\frac{(2j)!}{m!(2j-m)!}\;.

(243)

•

For tensors that are totally symmetric under exchanges of their indices we use the notation

t^{(\rho)}\equiv t^{\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{j}}\,.

(244)

•

Symmetrization and antisymmetrization are defined as

\displaystyle[a_{i},b_{j}]_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\left(a_{i}b_{j}\pm b_{j}a_{i}\right)\,,

\displaystyle[a_{i},b_{j}]

\displaystyle\equiv[a_{i},b_{j}]_{-}\,,

\displaystyle\{a_{i},b_{j}\}

\displaystyle\equiv[a_{i},b_{j}]_{+}\,.

(245)

•

The following notation for symmetrization over set(s) of labels or indices

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{(\rho)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\left\{\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\,,

(246a)

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{(\rho),(\sigma)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\left\{\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\underset{\left\{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\,,

(246b)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\vdots

For bi-indexed tensors (see Sec.
V.2.2
) we need symmetrizations over pairs of indices and we introduce the notation

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{(\mu\rho)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\left\{\mu_{1}\rho_{1},\cdots,\mu_{j}\rho_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\,,

(247a)

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{(\mu\rho),(\nu\sigma)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\left\{\mu_{1}\rho_{1},\cdots,\mu_{j}\rho_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\underset{\left\{\nu_{1}\sigma_{1},\cdots,\nu_{j}\sigma_{j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\,,

(247b)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\vdots

where symmetrizations like
{\mathcal{S}}_{\left\{(\mu\rho)\right\}}

are done treating the pairs of indices
\mu_{r}\rho_{r}
as one entity.
Notice that we do not introduce any normalization coefficient as part of the symmetrization definition. All coefficients will be explicitly stated in the equations.

•

For products, we use the empty product convention

\prod_{l=1}^{0}(\cdots)\equiv 1\,,

(248)

meaning that the product symbol with no factors is evaluated to one.

Appendix B
Helicity and light-front spinors

Besides the canonical spinors, which are constructed using rotationless boosts, there are other spinors in use in the literature. These are in particular the commonly used Jacob-Wick helicity spinors
[
42
]
and light-front helicity spinors
[
49
,
50
]
. Each of these spinors corresponds to a different choice of standard boost, which relates particles in their rest frame to moving frames. Each standard boost comes with an associated spin operator. Each common choice has different advantages that explains their use in relativistic quantum theory and their applications for multi-particle states. For detailed reviews see Refs.
[
37
,
51
]
and references therein. In this appendix, we summarize the main points relevant for the constructions presented in this work and how we can accommodate the formalism outlined in this article for helicity and light-front spinors.

The standard boosts
\Lambda_{\text{SB}}
are active transformations that connect rest frame states, which for massive particles are a state of a spin-
j
SU(2) irrep with a spin quantum number
\lambda
,
23
23

23

This exploits the semi-direct product structure of the Poincaré group to generate induced representations from the little group irreps
[
52
,
53
]
to a state with four momentum
p
and the
same
quantum number
\lambda
:

|p,j\lambda\rangle\equiv U[\Lambda_{\text{SB}}(\bm{p})]\,|\overset{\circ}{p},j\lambda\rangle\,.

(249)

These standard boosts
\Lambda_{\text{SB}}
usually correspond to one of the following choices

\displaystyle\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle=e^{-{\rm i}\rho\sum\limits_{m=1}^{3}\hat{p}_{m}\mathbb{K}_{m}}\,,

[canonical]

(250a)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{hel}}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle=e^{-{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\theta\mathbb{J}_{2}}e^{+{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\rho\mathbb{K}_{3}}\,,

[helicity]

(250b)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{hel'}}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle=e^{-{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\theta\mathbb{J}_{2}}e^{-{\rm i}\rho\mathbb{K}_{3}}\,,

[helicity
′
]

(250c)

\displaystyle\Lambda\text{LF}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle=e^{-{\rm i}\,\bm{p}_{\rm T}\cdot{\mathbb{G}}_{\rm T}}e^{-{\rm i}\omega\mathbb{K}_{3}}\,.

[light front]

(250d)

We remind that rapidity
\rho
was introduced in Eq. (
13
),
\theta
and
\phi
are the polar angles of final momentum
\bm{p}
. In the light-front standard boost of Eq. (
250d
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{\rm T}=(\mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2})\,,

(251a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{1}=\mathbb{K}_{1}+\mathbb{J}_{2}\,,

(251b)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{2}=\mathbb{K}_{2}-\mathbb{J}_{1}\,,

(251c)

\displaystyle e^{\omega}=\frac{p^{+}}{m}\,.

(251d)

The canonical standard boost uses only rotationless (pure) boosts. The helicity standard boost (due to Jacob and Wick) first has a boost along the
z
-axis to the full momentum
|\bm{p}|
followed by a rotation to the final direction. We list two possible rotations, differing by an initial rotation along the
z
-axis. The first corresponds to the original definition by Jacob & Wick
[
42
]
, whereas the second was adopted later. As exhibited by Eq. (
53
), the first, original, helicity form has certain advantages that allows it to be treated similarly to the light-front spinor parametrization in this formalism. The light-front standard boost, finally, is built only using light-front boosts
\mathbb{K}_{3},\mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2}
. A boost along the
z
-axis (up to the final light-front momentum component
p^{+}
) is followed by transverse light-front boosts.

Once a standard boost is chosen, single particle states
|p,j\lambda\rangle
– constructed by applying the standard boost to the rest frame states – furnish an infinitely-dimensional unitary representation of the Poincaré group. When boosting a moving single particle state with a Lorentz transformation, the state undergoes an additional momentum-dependent spin rotation (little group transformation), the so-called Wigner rotation
R_{\text{w}}
:

\displaystyle U[\Lambda]|p,j\lambda\rangle=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}|\Lambda p,j\lambda^{\prime}\rangle D^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}[R_{\text{w}}]\,,

(252)

\displaystyle R_{\text{w}}(\Lambda,\bm{p})=\Lambda_{\text{SB}}^{-1}(\Lambda\bm{p})\Lambda\Lambda_{\text{SB}}(\bm{p})\,,

(253)

see also Eq. (
23
) for the associated transformations of the annihilation operator.
The different choices of standard boost correspond to different Wigner rotations, each of which has their specific advantages
[
37
]
:

•

For the canonical spin, the Wigner rotation corresponding to a rotation
\Lambda=R
is that rotation,
R_{\text{w}}=R
. Consequently, canonical spin states are advantageous to work with when coupling spins in multi-particle states through the use of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.

•

For the helicity spin, the
\lambda
quantum number corresponds to the helicity projection of the canonical spin. Moreover, the Wigner rotation of
any
Lorentz transformation is a rotation around the
z
-axis. This conserves
\lambda
(meaning helicity is a Lorentz invariant) and the boosted state in Eq. (
252
) only acquires an extra phase.

•

For light-front spin, the light-front boosts form a kinematical subgroup
[
54
]
. As a result the Wigner rotation for any light-front boost is the identity
R_{\text{w}}=\mathbb{1}
. There are still non-trivial Wigner rotations for light-front rotations.

As all standard boosts take the rest frame momentum
\overset{\circ}{p}
to the same momentum
p
, different standard boost choices can be related by
momentum-dependent
rest frame rotations, the so-called generalized Melosh rotations
[
55
,
56
,
37
]
. The standard boosts can equivalently be written as a Melosh rotation
R_{\text{M}}
followed by the canonical standard boost:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{h}(\bm{p})=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})R_{\text{M}}[\text{h}]=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})\,e^{-{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\theta\mathbb{J}_{2}}e^{+{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}\,,

[helicity]

(254a)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{h^{\prime}}(\bm{p})=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})R_{\text{M}}[\text{h'}]=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})\,e^{-{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\theta\mathbb{J}_{2}}\,,

[helicity
′
]

(254b)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{LF}}(\bm{p})=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})R_{\text{M}}[\text{LF}]=\Lambda_{c}(\bm{p})\,e^{-{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}e^{-{\rm i}\theta_{\text{LF}}\mathbb{J}_{2}}e^{+{\rm i}\phi\mathbb{J}_{3}}\,,

[light front]

(254c)

where
\theta_{\text{LF}}=2\tan^{-1}\frac{p_{\rm T}}{p^{+}+m}
, and the Euler angles of the momentum-dependent Melosh rotation
R_{\text{M}}
can be read off on the right side of the equations. One observes that the Melosh rotation for both helicity standard boosts corresponds to the same rotations that appear after the
z
-axis boost in the standard boost of Eqs. (
250b
) and (
250c
). For both the original helicity and light-front cases, the Melosh rotations become trivial when the transverse momentum
p_{T}=0
(or
\theta=0
).

For the helicity and light-front choices of standard boosts, the bispinors of Eq. (
II.4
) appearing in the causal fields take the form

\displaystyle u_{h}(p,\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}\left[R_{z}(\phi)R_{y}(\theta)L(|\bm{p}|\hat{\bm{z}})\right]\,\overset{\circ}{u}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]\,\overset{\circ}{u}_{h}(\lambda)\,,

(255a)

\displaystyle u_{h^{\prime}}(p,\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}[R_{z}(\phi)R_{y}(\theta)R_{z}(-\phi)L(|\bm{p}|\hat{\bm{z}})]\,\overset{\circ}{u}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]\,\overset{\circ}{u}_{h^{\prime}}(\lambda)\,,

(255b)

\displaystyle u_{LF}(p,\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}\left[\exp\left(-{\rm i}\,\bm{p}_{\rm T}\cdot\mathbb{G}_{\rm T}\right)L(|\bm{p}|\hat{\bm{z}})\right]\,\overset{\circ}{u}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}^{(j)}[L(\bm{p})]\,\overset{\circ}{u}_{LF}(\lambda)\,,

(255c)

where in the
final equalities
we exploited Eq. (
254
) to introduce Melosh rotated rest-frame bispinors and spinors

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{u}_{i}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\overset{\circ}{u}_{iL,c}(\lambda)\\
\overset{\circ}{u}_{iR}^{\dot{d}}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(256a)

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{u}_{iL,c}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=m^{j}\mathchoice{\left(D^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\left(D^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}_{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=m^{j}\mathchoice{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}[R_{M}[i]]\right)^{{{\dot{d}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77779pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}=\overset{\circ}{u}_{iR}^{\dot{d}}(\lambda)\,,

(256b)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pti\in\{h,h^{\prime},\text{LF}\}\,.

Equivalent expressions can be written for the negative-energy spinors (or Eq. (
82
) can be applied).

The spinors have the following properties for any spin

•

The canonical bi-spinors transform well under parity
[see Eqs. (
69
), (
II.4
)]

\displaystyle\beta u(p,\lambda)=u(\bar{p},\lambda)\,,

(257a)

\displaystyle\beta v(p,\lambda)=(-)^{2j}v(\bar{p},\lambda)\,,

(257b)

where
\bar{p}=(E_{p},-\bm{p})
.
As can be seen from Eqs. (
51
) and (
52
), only the canonical bi-spinors transform in this simple manner, owning to the
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
being real valued. For any other choice of standard-boost parameterization, characterized by complex parameters
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
due to rotations, the bi-spinors are not eigenvectors of the parity operator.

•

The helicity spinors are eigenvectors of the helicity operator

\frac{W^{0}}{|\bm{p}|}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}&0\\
0&\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}^{(j)}\end{array}\right)\,,

(258)

where we introduced the Pauli-Lubanski vector

\displaystyle W^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\mathsf{M}_{\nu\rho}p_{\sigma}=-j\,\frac{\text{i}}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\mathcal{G}_{1,\nu\rho}p_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=j\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\text{red}}^{\mu\sigma\alpha}&0\\
0&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\mu\sigma\alpha}\end{array}\right)p_{\sigma}t^{(j)}_{\alpha 0\cdots 0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\text{red}}^{\mu\sigma\alpha}&0\\
0&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{Q}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{red}}^{\mu\sigma\alpha}\end{array}\right)p_{\sigma}J^{(j)}_{\alpha}\,,

where
\mathsf{M}^{\mu\nu}
are the Lorentz generators for the bispinor representation, and we used Eq. (
267
).
We have

\displaystyle\frac{W^{0}}{|\bm{p}|}\,u_{h}(p,\lambda)=\lambda\,u_{h}(p,\lambda)\,,

(263a)

\displaystyle\frac{W^{0}}{|\bm{p}|}\,v_{h}(p,\lambda)=-\lambda\,v_{h}(p,\lambda)\,,

(263b)

and similar relations for the
u_{h^{\prime}},v_{h^{\prime}}
spinors.

•

The light-front spinors are eigenvectors of the light-front helicity operator

\frac{W^{+}}{p^{+}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{p^{R}}{p^{+}}J^{(j)}_{L}+J^{(j)}_{3}&0\\
0&\frac{p^{L}}{p^{+}}J^{(j)}_{R}+J^{(j)}_{3}\end{array}\right)\,,

(264)

and we have

\displaystyle\frac{W^{+}}{p^{+}}\,u_{LF}(p,\lambda)=\lambda\,u_{LF}(p,\lambda)\,,

(265a)

\displaystyle\frac{W^{+}}{p^{+}}\,v_{LF}(p,\lambda)=-\lambda\,v_{LF}(p,\lambda)\,.

(265b)

Appendix C
A second algorithm for
t
-tensor construction

In this appendix, we present an alternative algorithm to construct the
t
-tensors. From a viewpoint of obtaining the final results, it is less efficient than the one presented in Sec.
III
. It does, however, provide more of a link with the underlying Lorentz group structure. The algorithm is iterative and makes use of the exponential map of Eq. (
33
), the traceless and symmetric nature the
t
-tensors, and the fact that elements of the
t
-tensors with
k
spatial indices are homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
in the generators of rotations
\bm{J}^{(j)}
.

The algorithm proceeds as follows:

1.

As noted before, the first term in the expansion of the matrix exponential Eq. (
33
), corresponding to the zeroth degree polynomial in
\bm{J}
, is always the identity

\displaystyle t^{0\ldots 0}=\mathbb{1}\,.

(266)

2.

The linear polynomials are obtained from the elements with one spatial index, and are proportional to the rotation group generators

\displaystyle t^{0\ldots i\ldots 0}=\frac{2}{2j}J_{i}=\frac{1}{j}J_{i}\,,

(267)

where the factor
1/2j
is needed to cancel the contributions from all
2j
elements of the symmetric tensor with exactly one spatial index.

3.

For the elements with exactly two spatial indices, we have to consider homogeneous polynomials in
\bm{J}
of degree 2. As the commutators of two
\bm{J}
reduce to a linear function in
\bm{J}
by making use of the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
algebra, we only have to consider anticommutators
\{J_{m},J_{n}\}
.

We form the elements
t^{0\ldots m\ldots 0\ldots n\ldots 0}
in such a way that they respect the traceless condition
t^{\ldots\mu\ldots\nu\ldots}g_{\mu\nu}=0
. To fix that traceless condition, we make explicit use of the quadratic Casimir of the rotation group

\left\{J_{k},J_{l}\right\}\delta_{kl}=2j(j+1)\;\mathbb{1}\,.

(268)

We can now write

\displaystyle t^{0\ldots m\ldots 0\ldots n\ldots 0}=t^{mn0\ldots 0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(2j)!}{2!(2j-2)!}\right)^{-1}\left(\left\{J_{m},J_{n}\right\}-\frac{1}{3}\left\{J_{r},J_{r}\right\}\delta_{mn}\right)+\frac{1}{3}t^{0\ldots 0}\delta_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j(2j-1)}\left(\left\{J_{m},J_{n}\right\}-\frac{2}{3}j(j+1)\mathbb{1}\delta_{mn}\right)+\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{1}\delta_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j(2j-1)}\left(\left\{J_{m},J_{n}\right\}-j\mathbb{1}\delta_{mn}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{j}{(2j-1)}\left(\left\{t^{m0\ldots 0},t^{n0\ldots 0}\right\}-\frac{1}{j}t^{0\ldots 0}\delta_{mn}\right)\,.

(269)

We divide by the binomial factor
\frac{(2j)!}{2!(2j-2)!}

to avoid overcounting permutations of equivalent indices in the symmetric tensor.
Because the traceless condition applies to the 4-index Lorentz indices, the
t^{mn0\ldots 0}
as a Cartesian rank 2 tensor is not traceless

\displaystyle t^{mm0\ldots 0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j(2j-1)}\left(2j(j+1)-3j\right)\mathbb{1}=\mathbb{1}\,.

(270)

4.

For
t
-tensor elements with a higher number of spatial indices, symmetrizations of higher polynomials of
J
s are treated recursively in a similar fashion. This demonstrates the link between the elements of the
t
-tensor and the Jordan algebra, built out of anticommutators of the
\bm{J}
. Contrary to the commutators (Lie algebra), this Jordan algebra is representation (spin) specific. As in Eq. (
269
), these can be related to expressions with tensor elements having fewer spatial indices. For elements with three spatial indices, we obtain for instance

\displaystyle t^{mnl0\ldots 0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(2j)!}{3!(2j-3)!}\right)^{-1}\frac{2}{9}\frac{1}{3!}\underset{\left\{m,n,l\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\left(J_{m}J_{n}J_{l}-(3j-1)\delta_{mn}J_{l}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt=\frac{j}{(2j-2)}\frac{1}{3}\left[\left(t^{mn0\ldots 0}t^{l0\ldots 0}+t^{nl0\ldots 0}t^{m0\ldots 0}+t^{lm0\ldots 0}t^{n0\ldots 0}\right)-\frac{1}{j}\left(\delta^{mn}t^{l0\ldots 0}+\delta^{nl}t^{m0\ldots 0}+\delta^{lm}t^{l0\ldots 0}\right)\right]\,,

(271)

where the last three terms fix the traceless conditions.

5.

Applying the Cayley-Hamilton theorem, the matrix
J_{\hat{\bm{p}}}\equiv\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{J}
satisfies its own characteristic equation. This, for the spin-
j
case, is expressed as

(J_{\hat{\bm{p}}}-j)(J_{\hat{\bm{p}}}-j-1)...(J_{\hat{\bm{p}}}+j)=0\,.

(272)

As a consequence, any polynomial in
J_{\hat{\bm{p}}}
of degree
>2j
can be reduced to one of maximal degree
2j
.
Hence, the algorithm finishes after
2j
iterations and the following set is generated

\displaystyle t^{0\ldots 0}\,,

\displaystyle t^{m_{1}0\ldots 0}\,,

\displaystyle t^{m_{1}m_{2}0\ldots 0}\,,

\displaystyle t^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}0\ldots 0}\,,

\displaystyle t^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\ldots m_{2j}}\,.

(273)

The
n
-th element of this set,
t_{m_{1}\cdots m_{n}0\cdots 0}
, is a 3-dimensional rank-
n
symmetric tensor, which satisfies
\frac{n!}{2!(n-2)!}
constraints.
It means that for element
n
there are
\frac{(n+2)!}{2!n!}-\frac{n!}{2!(n-2)!}=(2n+1)
independent matrices contained in
t_{m_{1}\cdots m_{n}0\cdots 0}
. The entire set has in total

\sum_{n=0}^{2j}(2n+1)=(2j+1)^{2}\,,

(274)

independent Hermitian matrices up to
2j
-degree polynomials in
J
s. This is a sufficient basis to generate the exponential map of Eq. (
33
).

With regards to the general shape of these matrices with more and more spatial indices, we have the following pattern emerging.

1.

The
t^{0\ldots 0}
is diagonal (unit matrix).

2.

The
t^{m_{1}0\ldots 0}
can be written as linear combinations of
J_{3}
and the raising and lowering operators
J_{\pm}
, so will have non-zero elements on the diagonal and the first off-diagonals.

3.

The
t^{m_{1}m_{2}\ldots m_{k}0\ldots 0}
with
k
spatial indices will have non-zero matrix elements up to the
k
-th off-diagonal, or non-zero matrix elements occur only in elements (
i,i-j
) and (
i-j,i
), for
j\in[0,k]
.

This illustrates that in order to describe matrix elements with
k
units of spin flip, at least the
t
-tensor matrix elements with
k
spatial indices are needed (corresponding to the
k
-th multipole, see Sec
V.3
).

Appendix D
Proof of the cubic reduction formula

In this section the
t
-tensor cubic reduction formula is proved to be given by

t^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2j}}\bar{t}^{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{2j}}t^{\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{2j}}=\frac{1}{[(2j)!]^{2}}\underset{\left\{(\nu),(\rho)\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{2j}{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}\rho_{l}\tau_{l}}\right)t_{\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{2j}}\,,

(275)

with

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu\nu\rho\tau}=g^{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\tau}-g^{\mu\rho}g^{\nu\tau}+g^{\mu\tau}g^{\nu\rho}+{\rm i}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\tau}\,.

(276)

We start with the known spin-1/2 identity

\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}\sigma^{\rho}=g^{\mu\rho}\sigma^{\rho}-g^{\mu\rho}\sigma^{\nu}+g^{\nu\rho}\sigma^{\mu}+{\rm i}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\tau}\sigma_{\tau}=\mathcal{C}^{\mu\nu\rho\tau}\sigma_{\tau}\,.

(277)

For the higher spin cases, we apply Eq. (
87
) three times to reduce the cubic product
t\bar{t}t
to products of all spin-1/2
\sigma
and
\bar{\sigma}
multiplied by towers of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients:

(278)

For the spinor indices, we reserve Roman indices
a,\ldots,f
for the contractions with spin-1/2 objects and
p,\ldots,u
Roman indices for indices that appear on the left side or are contracted between CG coefficients (and refer to higher-spin indices). At the end of this section, we will show that the last line of the previous equation reduces to

(280)

Using these identities in Eq. (
278
), each term in the two symmetrizations of Eqs. (
279
) and (
280
) generates
(2j)!
matrix products of triples
\sigma^{\mu_{i}}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu_{j}}\sigma^{\rho_{k}}
. Between the different terms, the symmetrization of the
spinor
indices
\{(e)\}
and
\{(\dot{b})\}
carries over into a symmetrization in
Lorentz
indices
\{(\nu)\},\{(\rho)\}
, meaning
all
possible combinations
\{\mu_{i},\nu_{j},\rho_{k}\}
of matrix products appear in the sums. In a final step, the spin-1/2 matrix products can then be reduced using Eq. (
277
). These steps lead to

Finally, using Eq. (
87
) to rebuild the
t
-tensor, we arrive at the desired Eq. (
275
).

What remains is to prove Eq. (
279
). From Eq. (
278
) and the transformation properties of the
t
-tensors [Eqs. (
39
)] and CG coefficients [Eqs. (
46
)], we can infer that
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}
is an invariant tensor in its spin-1/2 spinor indices. The symmetry in the Lorentz indices of the
t
-tensors, combined with the fact that spinor indices of
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}
are contracted with identical
\sigma
or
\bar{\sigma}
means that any permutation within spinor indices
\{d_{i}\}
and
\{e_{i}\}
can be compensated by a permutation of Lorentz indices and
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}
has to be completely symmetric in both sets of spin-1/2 spinor indices. As an antisymmetric invariant tensor, the Levi-Civita
\epsilon^{ij}
cannot be used to construct
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}
and the only available invariant SL(2,
\mathbb{C}
) tensor is
\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}_{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.79411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.62968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

(2j)!

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2j)!}\left(\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{1}}}}_{{{e_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{1}}}}_{{{e_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{1}}}}_{{{e_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{1}}}}_{{{e_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\cdots\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.17323pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.52135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{2j}}}}_{{{e_{2j}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.44205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.71336pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.17323pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.52135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{2j}}}}_{{{e_{2j}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.44205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.71336pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.17323pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.52135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{2j}}}}_{{{e_{2j}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.44205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.71336pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.17323pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.52135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d_{2j}}}}_{{{e_{2j}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.44205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.71336pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\right)\underset{\left\{e_{1}\cdots e_{2j}\right\}}{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e_{1}}}}_{{{d_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e_{1}}}}_{{{d_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e_{1}}}}_{{{d_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.11287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.80038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0717pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e_{1}}}}_{{{d_{1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.74411pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53157pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.87968pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\cdots\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.41121pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.44205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.71336pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e_{2j}}}}_{{{d_{2j}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.04245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.17323pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.52135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoic
